@@25615845 <h> By <h> By <h> COMMENTS <h> Share <h> COMMENTS <h> Related Content <p> The scenes in Japan in the wake of torrential rain that has caused landslides and widespread flooding are heartbreaking . The rains have been described as unprecedented , and the death toll has continued to rise as emergency workers and volunteers search for those who are still missing . <p> While some people have been able to return to their homes , many of the people who were ordered to evacuate remain displaced . Prime Minister Shinzo Abe 's government has committed millions of dollars to aid recovery . <p> The planet has already warmed . To continue to ignore or downplay this should no longer be acceptable . <p> Japan has experienced more than its fair share of disasters , including the devastating tsunami in 2011 that caused the meltdown at the Fukushima Daiichi Nuclear Power Plant . Scenes this week of flooded landscapes with the roofs of houses just visible above the water are eerily reminiscent of the horrific aftermath of the 2011 tsunami . <p> However , the recent rain and flooding have different lessons for us . Extreme weather events such @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a result of climate change and the continued warming of the planet . Heatwaves , bush fires , intense typhoons and cyclones , sea-level rise , flooding , and drought will all increase in both intensity and frequency unless we keep global warming below 1.5 -- 2 degrees Celsius . Even if we keep global warming below 2degrees we are still likely to face a significantly less stable or predictable climate than the one most of us grew up with . <p> In response to extreme heat and precipitation events across Asia , Europe , America , and the Middle East during June and July , the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change stated that : <p> Episodes of extreme heat and precipitation are increasing as a result of climate change . Although it is not possible to attribute the individual extreme events of June and July to climate change , they are compatible with the general long-term trend due to rising concentrations of greenhouse gases . <p> In the same week as the disaster in Japan , record hot temperatures were registered across California , with Disneyland soaring @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the state of New South Wales continued to be affected by one of the worst droughts in recent history . In Muscat , Oman , in June , the temperature did n't drop below 42 degrees for more than 24 hours . Reports of record-breaking extremes and violent " once in a hundred year " storms are becoming commonplace . <h> We need to talk about climate change <p> In media coverage of all these events , climate change is only occasionally mentioned , and often just in passing . There is a reluctance to talk about climate change in the wake of tragedy , particularly when linking a specific weather event to climate change is complex . Establishing a direct causal link involves complex scientific modelling and analysis of data , with results that are often nuanced rather than clear-cut . <p> The conclusion is often that while floods and droughts have always occurred , climate change is making their occurrence more frequent . Yet the argument over whether or not this particular drought or that particular storm was directly and indisputably caused by the warming planet is counterproductive @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the conversation away from a debate about climate change that all too often becomes politicised either though omission or oversimplification . We must focus on what these events can teach us about the kinds of climate-related risks we face in the near future , and how unprepared we are for them . <p> The tragedy in Japan should serve as a particularly alarming indicator of the kinds of challenges even the most disaster-prepared country faces . Japan has a highly developed disaster early-warning system , one that was utilised before the worst of the flooding and landslides caused so much devastation . <p> Early analysis suggests that a combination of urban development and land use in floodprone areas ; human complacency in the face of evacuation orders , or an inability to evacuate safely ; the sudden onset of torrential rains ; and Japan 's unique geography interacted in such a way that the human toll has been unacceptably high . <p> The Center for Climate and Security recently released a report titled A Responsibility to Prepare . It argues that we face a future of unprecedented risks as a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ unique capacity to predict some of these risks and prepare to deal with them . <p> This will involve more than just early-warning systems or financial aid to rebuild after disasters . It will involve paying attention to the unanticipated consequences of climate change and the human and institutional factors that make resilience or adaptability possible . As researcher Joshua Busby has argued : <p> the disruption to the Earth 's climate will ultimately command more attention and resources and have a greater influence on the global economy and international relations than other forces visible in the world today . <p> Our first responsibility should be mitigation . The Paris Agreement must be upheld , and strengthened . Failure to radically cut global carbon emissions will mean disasters such as the one unfolding in Japan will become the new normal . <p> Our second responsibility is to learn from the past and present , and be prepared for a future in which extreme weather events will challenge even the wealthiest , most developed states , and will be devastating for the poorest and least developed . The global climate is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ continue to ignore or downplay this should no longer be acceptable . 
@@25629077 <h> ' Serious questions ' over whether Australia 's emissions cuts are real <p> Methods used to pledge at least $1.15 billion in taxpayer-funded emissions cuts are under a cloud as officials probe whether the environmental benefits credited to the Coalition government 's flagship climate policy are genuine . <p> A former government official has told Fairfax Media that the scheme , the emissions reduction fund , has rightly focused on attracting participants to carbon-reducing projects such as revegetation , rather than assessing if the projects accurately estimated how much carbon was kept out of the atmosphere . <p> But critics say a lack of probity in the $2.55 billion Abbott-era policy mean it is impossible to know how much carbon reduction has actually occurred , casting doubt on the integrity of Australia 's contribution to international climate action . <p> Critics say promised cuts to carbon emissions under the federal government 's central climate policy may not be genuine . <p> Photo : MICK TSIKAS <p> The Clean Energy Regulator would not guarantee that all emissions reduction claimed under the scheme was genuine , but says it has " processes in place to address this risk " . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climate policy . It was introduced by the Abbott government in 2014 after Labor 's so-called " carbon tax " was repealed . <p> Advertisement <p> The fund gives financial incentives to businesses , landowners and others who reduce carbon emissions through projects such as planting trees , installing more efficient appliances or managing bushfires . <p> About half the 192 million tonnes of emissions cuts pledged under the fund relate to projects using one of two revegetation methods which together have promised abatement worth at least $1.15 billion . <p> The projects are mostly on grazing land in northwest NSW and southwest Queensland . They involve regrowing native forest by means such as limiting cattle grazing and managing feral animals . <p> In theory , this revegetation reduces greenhouse gas levels by drawing out carbon dioxide from the atmosphere and storing it as carbon in plants as they grow . <p> A government-appointed expert committee is examining the two methods in what it says is a routine review to ensure emissions reductions " remain genuine " . <p> A former official at the regulator , Raphael Wood , told @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ imagery of project areas that revealed " issues " with estimations of carbon abatement . He said it showed some land slated for revegetation likely had little forest potential , while forest may have already existed on other land where revegetation had been promised . <p> Mr Wood left the regulator in 2015 and is now an independent consultant . He said the onus was on project proponents to accurately measure carbon abatement and any miscalculation was probably not intentional . However the regulator needed to " tighten up the robustness of the standards " used to estimate carbon reserves . <p> " The regulator is transitioning from a period of participation focus to attract projects to the scheme to a compliance focus , " he said , adding the approach taken so far involved " learning by doing " . <p> Projects are issued with Australian carbon credit units as emission reduction is delivered . More than 12 million credits , each representing one tonne of carbon abatement , have been issued to projects using the methods that are under a cloud . Credits are mostly purchased by the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been held so far . <p> Fairfax Media is aware of concerns held by other former regulator staff that projects using the methods under review continue to be approved and issued with carbon credits , despite questions over their robustness . Such fears were underlined by the Climate Change Authority 's review of the fund in December last year , which cited concerns including that " vegetation on the ground may not match assumptions in the model " . <p> Mr Wood said while some claimed carbon abatement may not have actually been achieved , other projects had created more emissions cuts than they were credited for , or would do so in future . @@25646026 <h> The vital work Taronga and Western Plains Zoos do for species at risk <p> 15 July 2018 -- 12:00am <p> Even in an era of ubiquitous screens and the availability of 24 hour digital entertainment there is a unique pleasure to be found in visiting Taronga and Western Plain Zoos . While not having the instantly recognizable iconic status of the Opera House and the Harbour Bridge , the harbourside zoo is deeply woven into the fabric of this city . <p> Corroboree Frog at Taronga Zoo . Part of a near extinct animals replenishment program . <p> Photo : Taronga Zoo/Gary Ramage <p> And nothing quite lights up a child 's eyes like getting close to living creatures which he or she may have only seen previously in books , cartoons or nature programs . That joy is infectious , able to reawaken our own capacity for wonder at the myriad forms which life on earth can take . <p> But zoos do more than provide entertainment for curious humans . They are centres of research , and scientific inquiry and of education about the importance of preserving animal habitats . They do vital work in helping @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hope left for creatures at risk . <p> Taronga Zoo is at the forefront of that work , spending more than $10 million annually on a range of conservation , biodiversity and educational and awareness raising projects . <p> Among those programs is the work it is doing to help preserve the splendidly coloured Southern Corroboree frog from extinction . This startlingly beautiful amphibian is now thought to number no more than 50 individuals in its native habitat in the Kosciuszko National Park , and its survival is heavily dependent on specially designed refuges in the park . <p> Advertisement <p> Another beneficiary of the Zoo 's species preservation work is the bilby , now thought to be extinct in New South Wales . At Western Plains , near Dubbo , Taronga is trying to create a breeding base from which to reintroduce the animals . <p> It wants to use its 1200-hectare spread to create " a centre for conservation excellence " with a focus on NSW and and semi-arid zone species . Federal and state governments contribute to this work as well , with tens of millions allocated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ project over the next 5 years . <p> Such work is vital . According to the Threatened Species Recovery Hub , a research program shared among a number of universities and the Australian Wildlife Conservancy , up to ten Australian birds and seven mammals are like to become extinct over the next 20 years while fifty or more species remain at risk . <p> Programs to breed threatened species in captivity are like modern day equivalents of Noah 's Ark . They provide a haven for populations which have shrunk to near-disappearance through loss of habitat , climate change , competition from introduced species , deforestation , urbanisation and loss of wilderness . <p> It is one reason why The Sun Herald and Fairfax Media are so proud to announce a new partnership with Taronga and Western Plains Zoos , to showcase their ground-breaking conservation work , and raise public awareness of and engagement with it . <p> But like the animals of the Ark , creatures bred to hopefully regenerate populations outside the protected space of zoos need healthy habitats to return to . Preservation of native vegetation in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ So too are steps like reducing the use of easily-discarded plastic into the environment . The state government continues to resist implementing a statewide plastic bag ban , despite some other states and territories taking that step . Once again , we urge the government to revisit that decision . Our native fauna and flora is priceless ; once gone , species are gone forever . It is our duty to preserve what we can for the generations to come .

@@25664126 <p> Renewable energy is on track to supply more than a third of the power in Australia 's National Electricity Market ( NEM ) by 2020 thanks to a combination of large-scale renewable projects and Australia 's booming rooftop solar industry . <p> New analysis also reveals that with higher emissions reduction targets , renewable energy could account for up to 85% of the National Electricity Market 's consumption by 2030 . The projections come from this month 's Renewable Energy Index , compiled by Green Energy Markets . <p> The REI reveals that solar and wind farms contracted or being built , combined with steady rooftop solar investment will contribute 33.3 per cent of power to the National Energy Market by 2020 . This almost doubles the 2015 rate when renewables contributed just 17.3 per cent of annual electricity consumption . <p> Even if contracting and construction commitments to solar farms and wind farms halted from today , ongoing installations of rooftop solar would still see the renewables share reaching 39 per cent by 2030 . This compares to around 36 per cent under the Turnbull Government 's National Energy Guarantee ( NEG ) . <p> In other @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the National Energy Guarantee if large-scale renewables collapse for a decade , and the growth of rooftop solar slows significantly . <p> GetUp Campaigns Director Miriam Lyons said with political leadership and vision , Australia could be a global leader in renewable energy and do its bit to meet Paris Climate Agreement commitments . <p> " The rapid ramp-up in investment in the past few years means we 're already on track to achieve massive increases in renewable energy and it demonstrates we can reach far stronger targets down the track . <p> " The biggest obstacle now is the Turnbull Government and its grossly inadequate National Energy Guarantee . @@25677082 <h> Aquatic news index now available online <p> ILOILO CITY -- A news index on aquatic and marine related news is now available online with the efforts of the library staff of the Southeast Asian Fisheries Development Center-Aquaculture Department ( SEAFDEC-AQD ) . <p> The Aquatic News Index ( ANI ) , which is being maintained by the SEAFDEC-AQD library , contains aquatic science-related newspaper articles . <p> They will also include two local newspapers , the Daily Guardian and the Panay News . <p> " We will try how to save our newspaper before it ends up as a wrapper for dried fish . We will try to index our newspaper and establish an online index for ease of retrieval and citation , " he said . <p> Alayon said they will try to analyze the news and even the use of jargons to comprehend the content of the publication . <p> He added that entries are grouped according to a newspaper source . <p> The subject description will be assigned using the Aquatic Sciences and Fisheries Abstract Thesaurus while the geographic names are assigned using the Getty Thesaurus of Geographic Names . <p> " We assigned subjects @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the extract , not the full text of it . So there is no file associated that they can download the article . But if the newspaper later on will grant us the permission to upload , much better , " he added . <p> Alayon said news reporters can benefit from index should they want to find historical news data , which contains the name of the publisher , subject , important names and agencies mentioned in the news and the writer . <p> " If you are a reporter and you want to search your profile in the Google Scholar , you will find the article and point you to the ANI ( ani.seafdec.org.ph ) . If you searches for a certain topic , you can find articles about the subject on the repository , " he said . <p> In addition to aquatic and marine sciences topics , the index also covers other topics like climate change and red tide , among others . <p> The index will try to cover publications from 1973 up to the present but will start with the recent ones . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ initiatives -- the Computerized Index to Philippine Periodicals and Index to Philippine Newspapers which was published by the University of the Philippines and Ateneo but is not indexed in Google Scholar . <p> The index was launched last week in time for the 45th founding anniversary of SEAFDEC . @@25679252 <h> Thawing permafrost microbiomes fuel climate change <p> 17 July 2018 <p> A University of Queensland-led international study could lead to more accurate predictions of the rate of global warming from greenhouse gas emissions produced by thawing permafrost in the next 100 years . <p> The study of the microorganisms involved in permafrost carbon degradation links changing microbial communities and biogeochemistry to the rise of greenhouse gas emissions . <p> " This is important as methane is a potent greenhouse gas - 25 times more efficient at trapping the sun 's radiation in our atmosphere than carbon dioxide . @@25710818 <h> Q&amp;A , the show that launched a million tweets , turns 10 <h> By Neil McMahon <p> It was the pilot -- taped May 8 , 2008 -- that launched a million tweets . Host ? Tony Jones . Guests ? Politicians from the major parties . Subject matter ? Sexual harassment . The more things change ... <p> Front and centre 10 years ago , when the ABC first corralled a panel under Jones 's whip and invited audience members to ask questions , was the scandal over chair-sniffing WA MP Troy Buswell . Also fresh , then and today : the guests on the panel . <p> There was Tony Abbott , who has since gone from gadfly backbencher to Opposition leader , to PM , and back to gadfly backbencher . Alongside was Labor 's Tanya Plibersek , in 2008 a newly minted minister under Kevin Rudd , who has also traversed both sides of the parliament in the decade since . Both remain front and centre in political debate , as does the TV experiment they called Q&amp;A . <p> In an exclusive joint interview to mark the 10th anniversary of the program , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ producer Peter McEvoy recalled its humble beginnings . <p> Q&amp;A presenter Tony Jones . <p> Photo : Wolter Peeters <p> Advertisement <p> The ABC wanted a discussion program ; McEvoy was charged with bringing it to life . Jones says he was on board early but insisted on one key factor : " I wanted it to be live and more or less made that a condition of being involved . " <p> Live it was and live it remains , with all the thrills and perils that entails . Looking back , Jones and McEvoy remember that pilot as an exciting if half-baked experiment . <p> " The striking thing about it now is that we were n't really ready , " McEvoy says . <p> " The set was n't finished so we did it in front of walls of brown paper , there was no Q&amp;A logo on the floor -- I think someone chalked one in ... " <p> Julia Gillard gave the show its best one-liner to date . <p> Jones : " We did have a real-live Tony Abbott and a real-live Tanya Plibersek @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people understood it and were straight into it ... after the program was finished my boss had a look and said , ' You 've got a program ' . It worked straight away . " <p> Q&amp;A producer Peter McEvoy and host Tony Jones . <p> Photo : Wolter Peeters <p> The first broadcast episode , on May 22 , 2008 , a one-on-one with Rudd , set the tone . It placed Q&amp;A at the centre of national debate . And it gave political leaders a platform to perform -- for the voters , for their colleagues and for the media -- for better and sometimes worse . <p> Jones and McEvoy have seen four PMs come and go -- and one come again : Rudd , Julia Gillard , Rudd again , Abbott and Malcolm Turnbull . <p> Malcolm Turnbull 's leather jacket -- iconic or ironic ? <p> As McEvoy recalls , Abbott became " our most frequent participant ... until he became prime minister " , when he became the program 's most virulent foe after the Zaky Mallah affair . ( Rudd was out of The Lodge four months later . ) <p> Tony Jones in action . <p> Photo : Wolter Peeters <p> PM Gillard " recognised that she did very well on the program " , says McEvoy . " And she delivered what he calls " the best Q&amp;A one-liner of all time " . <p> Jones picks up the story , quoting a question to Gillard : " On a scale of one to 10 , how big a tool is Mark Latham ? -- pause , pause , for a fraction of a second -- and then , ' Some things are beyond measure ' . " <p> Zaky Mallah 's controversial appearance on Q&amp;A in 2015 . <p> Photo : Eddie O'Reilly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ most frequent guest , whose appearance in a leather jacket has become an iconic -- and ironic -- reflection of his positioning as the great progressive hope in the Abbott era . <p> Did Turnbull use Q&amp;A as a marketing tool ? <p> Jones : " You could say he used it , or I 'd prefer to say he used it as a way to raise his public profile and to show his depth across a whole range of subjects and he did that very well . " <p> The program has just as often been a forum for ordinary people to command the national stage . <p> There was Zaky Mallah , whose question about terrorism laws embroiled Q&amp;A in the defining scandal of its decade on air . " Ridiculously overblown , " says McEvoy of that 2015 uproar . Jones recalls being depicted in a cartoon as a severed head in the storm that followed . " Just a head , having been decapitated . " The power of one , " says Jones of that show-stopping moment . " Every now and then they present the best moments of the program . " <p> There have been special-issue programs -- on disability , Shakespeare , the Magna Carta -- topics that can appear left-field but often produce great television . " We want to surprise people @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> And there has been Twitter : Q&amp;A pioneered live engagement with the Twitter-sphere and it has become an integral part of the program . McEvoy calls it " a perfect meeting between a live program ... and a medium that 's about short , pithy live comments about live events . Those two things came together and it did n't exist when we first started " . <p> Both Jones and McEvoy maintain Q&amp;A remains relevant , and still ripe for reinvention as it enters its second decade . @@25713021 <p> Over the last three decades the Great Barrier Reef has been hit by a series of intense cyclones , bleaching , crown of thorn starfish outbreaks and flood events that have caused well-documented , but reparable damage . <p> Scientists have hoped that an extended period of benign conditions would allow the natural processes of reef restoration to flourish , and many of the hardest-hit regions to return to a healthier , more colourful and biodiverse state . <p> Branching Acropora coral was particularly slow to recover following disturbance . <p> Branching Acropora coral was particularly slow to recover following disturbance . <p> AIMS : Roger Steene <p> But a new study of coral-recovery rates based on 18 years of data and published in Science Advances today , found the ability of many corals to bounce back after disturbance has significantly slowed down . <p> Although recovery rates were variable between different reef patches and coral types , the researchers found the overall recovery rate of corals across the Great Barrier Reef declined by an average of 84 per cent between 1992 and 2010 . <p> Following acute disturbance events like cyclones , coral recovery was hindered by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ author Juan-Carlos Ortiz from the University of Queensland . <p> " We noticed that ... water quality played a significant role in that reduction in recovery rate , " Dr Ortiz said . <p> The study , supported by the Australian Institute of Marine Science ( AIMS ) and the Nature Conservancy , looked at data from more than 90 primarily mid-shelf and offshore reefs , comparing the rate of recovery following disturbance events . <p> " We noticed for the first time a very large decline in the ability of the reef to recover from disturbances over those 18 years , " he said . <p> The research team classified corals into six groups based on their growth forms . Although all groups showed an overall decline in recovery rate , two groups ? ? " the Montipora and branching Acropora both " went into negative " . <p> What that means is they continued to decline even after the disturbance had ceased . <p> While increased disturbance events are expected as the impacts of climate change ramp up , the slowed recovery time is a concerning compounding factor @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ assumption that we were working on was that naturally reefs recover fast , " Dr Ortiz said . <p> Some coral types had close to zero recovery , whereas others showed high recovery rates . <p> Some coral types had close to zero recovery , whereas others showed high recovery rates . <p> Supplied : Peter Mumby <p> Although the results paint a grim picture of the trajectory of the reef , the researchers say there are some very positive things that can be taken from their findings . <p> The reefs furthest offshore receive less runoff from the catchment area , and because they are generally buffered by deeper water , are less susceptible to short-term fluctuations in temperature . <p> Although the study only analysed data from up until 2010 , Dr Ortiz said there was a period without significant disturbance to the reef following Cyclone Yasi in 2011 . <p> " On the offshore reefs like the Swains , where they are least affected by water quality issues from land ... they did recover really fast , " he said . <p> " Which suggests that this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ vital <p> In short , the researchers said both improving the water quality of runoff from the reef catchment area and addressing climate change can help reverse the reef 's decline . <p> But trying to improve conditions on the reef without tackling climate change is like putting " band-aids on arterial wounds " , according to James Cook University 's Associate Professor Jodie Rummer , who was not involved in the study . <p> " We definitely need to be controlling problems with water quality and problems with crown of thorns , but first and foremost we need to deal with the big problem , " Dr Rummer said . <p> " What it does come down to is warming . Everything else just makes it worse , but warming is the primary concern . " <p> Dr Rummer , who will be presenting some of her work at a two-day reef symposium in Brisbane this week , has been studying sharks in French Polynesia and on the Great Barrier Reef . <p> Although French Polynesia is a declared shark sanctuary , she said their numbers are still suffering @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from the Queensland Audit Office shows more than 1.2 million hectares were cleared in Queensland between 2012 and 2016 , and nearly 40 per cent of that was cleared in the reef catchment area . <p> Despite committing $500 million to protecting the reef in the budget , the Federal Government came under fire earlier this year when it granted approval for the clearing of 2,000 hectares of bushland at Kingvale Station , which drains into reef-fringed Princess Charlotte Bay in North Queensland . <p> This week , the Federal Government committed $3.5 million to help sugarcane farmers " improve fertiliser use and efficiency " in the catchment . @@25713480 <h> Great Barrier Reef fears as coral recovery slows significantly <p> The Great Barrier Reef is nowhere near as resilient as it once was and it has experts concerned for its future . Source : JCU <p> The Great Barrier Reef is nowhere near as resilient as it once was and it has experts concerned for its future . <p> UpdatedUpdated 19 July <p> Share <p> Researchers are concerned about the Great Barrier Reef 's powers of recovery from bleaching , poor water quality and disturbances . <p> Scientists at the University of Queensland and the Australian Institute of Marine Science ( AIMS ) have found a decline in the ability of the reef to recover after bleaching , outbreaks of crown of thorns starfish or cyclones from 1992 to 2010 . <p> Dr Juan Ortiz , lead author from AIMS and UQ 's school of biological sciences , says that during this time , average coral recovery rates showed a sixfold decline across the Great Barrier Reef . <p> Weather over coming months will determine whether the Great Barrier Reef is hit by coral bleaching . <p> The frequency of acute disturbances is predicted to increase , making @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ first time a decline in recovery rate of this magnitude has been identified in coral reefs , " he said <p> " The decline is driven by a combination of the legacy effect of acute disturbances . The future of the Great Barrier Reef is threatened without further local management to reduce chronic disturbances and support recovery , and strong global action to limit the effect of climate change . " <p> UQ Professor Peter Mumby , of the Centre of Excellence for Coral Reef Studies , said there was serious cause for concern but it was important to stress that not all reefs were failing . <p> " I believe there is scope for management to help remedy the situation , " he said . <p> " Our results indicate that coral recovery is sensitive to water quality and is suppressed for several years following powerful cyclones . <p> " Some reefs could improve their recovery ability if the quality of the water entering the reef is actively improved . "

@@25831557 <p> In one of several speeches warning of the demise of the Australian Labor Party , former Labor elder John Faulkner cautioned against what was , at the time , the party 's cult of changing leaders . <p> Faulkner was delivering the annual Neville Wran lecture in June 2011 . It was a year after Kevin Rudd was deposed during his first term as prime minister , and three months after NSW Labor was tossed out of government by voters sick of its merry-go-round of leadership changes and ineptitude . <p> The " NSW disease " , as Rudd coined it on his fateful day , was entrenched in the party at a state and federal level . <p> Faulkner 's lament was that leadership changes had become regarded as an easy fix to problems that required far greater structural change . This included the need for bolder policies , which reflected belief and values , rather than the results of focus groups , and internal reforms to empower members and grow the party 's appeal . <p> Albanese is regarded as more genuine than Shorten . David Rowe <p> At the time , Labor 's poll ratings @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a year away from facing a return challenge by Rudd -- which failed but set the scene for his successful push the next year . <h> Related Quotes <p> " We have , at a state and a federal level , looked at our dropping support -- and more often than not , we have blamed the leader , " Faulkner said . <p> " And , in NSW , more often than not , we have changed the leader . <p> " It is time for the party to realise that there is more amiss here than any one individual can be asked to shoulder the blame for . It is time for us to realise that we have significant problems as an organisation and we must resolve them . " <p> Labor has made efforts since then on reforms . Rudd bequeathed the party new rules , which made it harder to dump a federal leader and gave the members an equal say in choosing a new one , if there were two or more candidates . <p> There have been token efforts at empowering members in other @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Bill Shorten has been one of the most vigorous oppositions in recent history in setting the policy agenda . <p> One could argue that the differences between the major parties as they currently stand are as stark as they have been for a long time , be it over income tax , company tax , negative gearing and , as the looming stoush over an emissions reduction target for the National Energy Guarantee suggests , climate change . <p> Still , there are significant elements within the party who feel this may not be enough and a leadership switcheroo may be required . <p> The national published polls show the contest has tightened and if the election becomes a beauty contest between Shorten and Malcolm Turnbull , Shorten would lose . <p> This is fuelling talk over whether to move to Anthony Albanese should Labor lose one or both of the seats of Longman and Braddon on Saturday . The view is by no means unanimous . <p> Even the ever-powerful NSW Right is divided over what to do should Saturday be a boilover . Some want change , others @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it 's too difficult . <p> The best outcome for Labor and Shorten would be to hold both seats relatively comfortably . <p> If not , there are several factors folks should consider before choosing the nuclear option . <p> As this column canvassed a few weeks ago , one unknown cost of change is the transactional cost . That is the destruction caused by the infighting that precedes and follows a leadership change . <p> Changing an opposition leader is not as an egregious act as dumping a prime minister . The latter betrays the will of the people , the former is simply an opposition party exercising its prerogative to put itself in the best possible position to win power . <p> Sometimes it works , as when Rudd deposed Kim Beazley in December 2006 . Often it can be messy . Even if the caucus manages to circumvent Rudd 's leadership rules , Shorten is unlikely to go quietly into the night . It will be a gamble . <p> As it stands , Albanese is regarded as more genuine than Shorten . Having been elected in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ he satisfies the 20-year rule . <p> In an act of mischief last weekend , the tabloids polled the key super Saturday seats and found Labor would hold both if Albanese was leader . <p> As interesting as these polls are , they are conducted in laboratory conditions , devoid of the travails and baggage that count against the actual leader at the time of the poll -- in this case , Shorten . <p> But should there be a change , the nice guy rapport the Liberals have with Albanese will vanish , as will any unbridled popularity . <p> The Liberal Party has done its due diligence and has an Albanese strategy ready to go should Labor change leaders . This includes , but is not limited to , him voting against turning around boats at Labor 's 2015 national conference , and supporting a Buffet tax , which would require people on very high incomes to pay a fixed percentage income tax . <p> The Coalition also contends that despite his popularity inside political circles and inner city areas , Albanese is not as well known across @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ canvas can be a plus and a minus . On the plus side , Albanese would have the opportunity to craft his own image . On the minus side , the Coalition would do it for him . <p> And as for internal stability , would Albanese be able to wrangle the unions as successfully as Shorten ? It 's an important part of the job . <p> Although he is from the Left , Albanese has proposed a more business-friendly approach than Shorten , even if that does not translate into support for the company tax cuts . He is also not afraid of making his case to the likes of Alan Jones and Andrew Bolt . <p> Given Turnbull appears determined to hold the election next year , even if a early poll was winnable , Albanese , if he became leader , may have to do something significant on policy to maintain any momentum generated by change . <p> Certainly the Coalition does not relish the prospect of a Labor leadership change , if only because Albanese is an unknown quantity . @@25835507 <h> World 's marine wilderness is dwindling <p> An international study led by University of Queensland scientists has found that only 13 per cent of the ocean can still be classified as wilderness . <p> Researchers from UQ 's School of Biological Sciences and international collaborators identified marine areas devoid of intense human impacts by analysing 19 stressors including commercial shipping , sediment runoff and several types of fishing . <p> PhD candidate Kendall Jones said most of the remaining marine wilderness was unprotected , leaving it vulnerable to being lost . <p> " Marine areas that can be considered pristine are becoming increasingly rare as fishing and shipping fleets expand their reach across almost all of the world 's oceans , and sediment runoff smothers many coastal areas , " Mr Jones said . <p> " Improvements in shipping technology mean that even the most remote wilderness areas may come under threat in the future , including once ice-covered places that are now accessible because of climate change . " <p> The researchers found little wilderness remaining in coastal habitats such as coral reefs , because of nearby human activities . <p> Most marine wilderness was located in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ such as French Polynesia . <p> " Marine wilderness areas are home to unparalleled levels of life , holding massive abundances of species and high genetic diversity , giving them resilience to threats like climate change , " Professor Watson said . <p> " We know these areas are declining catastrophically , and protecting them must become a focus of multilateral environmental agreements . <p> " If not , they will likely disappear within 50 years . " <p> Mr Jones said preserving marine wilderness also required regulating the high seas , which had proven difficult historically , as no country had jurisdiction . <p> " Late last year the United Nations began developing a legally binding high seas conservation treaty , essentially a Paris Agreement for the ocean , " he said . <p> " This agreement would have the power to protect large areas of the high seas and might be our best shot at saving some of Earth 's last remaining marine wilderness . " 
@@25835520 <p> Aussies may scoff at Britain 's idea of a heatwave , but this time it 's the real deal and it 's no laughing matter . <p> Extreme heat has hit locations throughout the Northern Hemisphere , in places as far apart as Montreal , Glasgow , Tokyo and Lapland . In the past few weeks heat records have been broken in a wide range of places , most notably : <p> Heat has not been the only problem . Much of northern Europe is experiencing a very persistent drought , with little to no measurable rainfall in months . This has caused the normally lush green fields of England and other European countries to turn brown and even reveal previously hidden archaeological monuments . <h> What 's behind the extreme heat ? <p> The jet stream , a high-altitude band of air that pushes weather systems around at lower altitudes , has been weaker than normal . <p> It has also been positioned unusually far to the north , particularly over Europe . This has kept the low-pressure systems that often drive wind and rain over northern Europe at bay . <p> The jet stream has remained @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and northern Europe for the past couple of months . This has meant that the same weather types have remained over the same locations most of the time . <p> Weather is typically more transient than it has been recently . Even when we do have blocking high-pressure systems associated with high temperatures in northern Europe , they do n't normally linger as long as this . <h> Is it driven by climate change ? <p> Although climatologists have made great strides in recent years in the field of event attribution -- identifying the human climate fingerprint on particular extreme weather events -- it is hard to quantify the role of climate change in an event that is still unfolding . <p> Until the final numbers are in we wo n't be able to tell just how much climate change has altered the likelihood or intensity of these particular heat extremes . <p> Having said that , we can use past analyses of extreme heat events , together with future climate change projections , to infer whether climate change is playing a role in these events . <p> For all @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Japan , a clear and discernible link with climate change has been made . <p> Research has also shown that heat extremes similar to those witnessed over the past month or two are expected to become more common as global temperatures continue to climb . The world has so far had around 1 ? of global warming above pre-industrial levels , but at the global warming limits proposed in the Paris climate agreement , hot summers like that of 2003 in central Europe would be a common occurrence . <p> Similarly , we know that heat exposure and heat-induced deaths in Europe will increase with global warming , even if we can limit this warming to the levels agreed in Paris . <h> Have n't summers always been hot ? <p> For most parts of the world summers have got warmer , and the hottest summer on record is relatively recent -- such as 2003 in parts of central Europe and 2010 in much of eastern Europe . <p> One exception is central England , where the hottest summer remains 1976 , although it may be challenged this year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the past , they were less common . One big difference as far as England is concerned is that its extreme 1976 heatwave was a global outlier , whereas this year 's is n't . <p> In 1976 north-western Europe had higher temperature anomalies than almost anywhere else on the globe . In June 2018 the same region was unusually warm , but so was most of the rest of the Northern Hemisphere . <p> So while the persistent weather patterns are driving much of the extreme heat we 're seeing across the Northern Hemisphere , we know that human-caused climate change is nudging the temperatures up and increasing the odds of new heat extremes . <p> Andrew King is ARC DECRA fellow and Ben Henley is research fellow in Climate and Water Resources at the University of Melbourne . This article originally appeared on The Conversation .

@@25869197 <h> Cuba to study whether climate change is hurting sugar harvests <p> Cuba plans to study whether sugar cane harvests like this one in Matanzas province have been hurt by climate change <p> Cuba is studying whether to adjust its sugar-harvest calendar in response to damaging changes in the island 's climate , an official newspaper reported Saturday . <p> The Caribbean island , once the world 's leading sugar producer , is suffering through a years-long decline in production even as its socialist leaders struggle to modernize a sluggish economy . <p> Cuban president Miguel Diaz-Canel ordered state sugar monopoly Azcuba to undertake the climate study in coordination with the Ministry of Science , Technology and the Environment , and to carry out any necessary remedial measures , the newspaper Granma said . <p> " The seasons are changing and we have to be able to adapt , " Diaz-Canel was quoted as saying . <p> He said the traditional sugar-growing season , lasting from November to April , might have to be adjusted . <p> The 2017-18 harvest produced " something more than a million tonnes of sugar , " an amount lower than expected and far @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ levels . <p> Azcuba 's new president , Julio Garcia Perez , said the changing climate was among the " fundamental causes " of lower production , with historically heavy rainfall affecting the quality of sugar cane . <p> But he also cited causes including " the low productivity of harvesting and transportation equipment ... and management deficiencies . " <p> Cuba has seen its industry decline for decades , due to reduced purchases from Russia and Eastern Europe , low global prices , lack of investment and a reduction in farmed land . <p> From an annual peak of seven million tonnes in the 1970s , the industry has in recent years struggled to manage exports of two million tonnes , even as world sugar prices have recovered . <p> According to Azcuba , the island has 56 sugar mills , 10 refineries and 12 distilleries . <p> A total of 588,667 hectares ( 1.45 million acres ) of land is devoted to sugar-cane cultivation . The state industry has a fleet of 1,363 harvesters , 7,833 tractors and 4,623 trucks . <p> Cuba plans to study whether sugar @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hurt by climate change 
@@25874634 <p> As the blazing sun beats down , combine harvesters working the normally fertile breadbasket of Saxony-Anhalt in former communist East Germany kick up giant clouds of dust as they roll over the cracked earth . <p> " It has n't really rained since April and that 's the main growth period for our grains and the other crops -- we 've never seen anything like it , " said Juliane Stein of Agro Boerdegruen , a farming conglomerate formed after German reunification in 1990 . <p> " We 've reached the point here in Germany where we 're talking about a natural disaster that 's a threat to our livelihood . " <p> A natural disaster is declared by German authorities during a drought when at least 30 percent of the average annual harvest is destroyed . <p> Given the massive losses feared by the sector this year , the German Farmers ' Association ( DBV ) has called crisis talks on Tuesday to discuss urgent state aid . <p> While southern Germany has seen largely normal rainfall this year , the north has been in the grip of an unrelenting high-pressure system creating weather conditions more familiar @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ alone has shrunk by up to eight million tonnes or around 18 percent this year , stripping 1.4 billion euros ( $1.6 billion ) from revenues so far . <p> " The government needs to declare a state of emergency so that farmers in areas hit hardest by the drought can be helped directly with cash aid , " Rukwied said . <p> While the sunshine has fostered larger and sweeter fruit than usual , sugar beets , rapeseed , potato and corn crops have been decimated in the drought , prompting farmers to cut their losses and harvest two to three weeks earlier than usual . <p> " The cornstalks are knee-high " and are sprouting smaller cobs or none at all , said Stein of Agro Boerdegruen , located about 150 kilometres ( 90 miles ) west of Berlin . <p> " Normally they should be over two metres by now . The number of slaughtered cows and heifers surged 10 percent in the first two weeks of July , according to figures from the Federal Agriculture and Nutrition Agency . <p> While Sweden and Greece have been ravaged by devastating forest fires , Germany has been less afflicted due to its less vulnerable types of vegetation and higher concentration of fire brigades . <p> However grain fields mark an exception and Saxony-Anhalt has seen wide swathes of farmland go up in flames . <p> " Wheat when it 's dry is as flammable as straw , " Stein said . <p> A drive through the farmland east of the River Elbe shows crops covered with the black soot of recent fires , with 70 hectares ( 170 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ remains of a spark that raged across the parched field this month . <p> - ' Nothing you can do ' - <p> So will northern Germany become a region of olives , wine and citrus fruits ? Stein said farmers here will likely find other ways to adapt . <p> No-till farming , which allows seeds to be sown without disturbing the soil , and the use of mulch to improve germination rates are two techniques already in practice . <p> Meanwhile seed breeding is developing crops that are more resistant to heat and dryness . However the process can take a decade or more and the European Court of Justice ruled last week it should be considered genetic manipulation and thus subject to stricter scrutiny by regulators . <p> Thomas Endrulat of the German Weather Service said it had been at least 15 years since the country had experienced a similarly hot , dry summer . <p> Such extremes matched forecasts seen in climate change models for Europe but he warned against drawing catastrophic conclusions from an " exceptional " year . <p> " You are seeing a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ winters or heavy rainfall and floods , " Endrulat told AFP . <p> " That bandwidth is part of our weather in central Europe . " <p> But for farmers in the grip of this year 's drought , that is cold comfort . <p> " You plant the grain in the autumn , it germinates and then it needs water in the spring to grow , " Stein said . " If that does n't happen , there 's nothing you can do . " the Australian Capital Territory . <p> By combining climate change policy and energy policy , the Turnbull Government believes the NEG can solve the vexing issues that have troubled Australian politics for more than a decade . <p> But the states and territories in the National Energy Market ( all except Western Australia and the Northern Territory ) need to agree . <p> The ACT Climate Change Minister Shane Rattenbury is the man in the middle . <p> " I think this is the great dilemma for the ACT : we think there are significant policy issues yet to be addressed , but we have to survive the politics of the coming few weeks of the NEG , " he said . <p> " That is ? ? ? the challenge that lies ahead of us . " <p> The NEG would force energy retailers to guarantee a portion of their power comes from dispatchable sources to ensure reliability , combined with an @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cent . <p> This would drive down electricity prices and provide investment certainty for the sector , according to its proponents . <p> Speaking at the Australian National University ( ANU ) , Mr Rattenbury outlined the four hurdles that stand between the ACT Government and signing on to the NEG. <h> 1 . The ambition of the target <p> The NEG aims to reduce emissions in the electricity sector by 26 per cent by 2030 ( based on the emissions levels in 2005 ) . <p> Mr Rattenbury points to modelling by industry analysts that shows emissions would drop by 24 per under the status quo , meaning the complicated policy would only cut emissions by 2 per cent over 10 years . <p> That provides little incentive for the energy market to invest in new renewable energy power sources . <p> " I think this points to the folly of the NEG , which is it will stymie renewables and it will stymie cheaper power coming into the system , " Mr Rattenbury said . <p> He 's worried that will leave a gap in new power sources @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ when several major coal-fired power stations are due to reach the end of their working lives . <p> " If they think there 's a reliability problem now , they ai n't seen nothing yet . " <h> 2 . Meeting the Paris agreement obligations <p> In 2015 , the Abbott government signed up to the Paris Climate Change Agreement , which compels Australia to reduce its greenhouse gas emissions by 26 per cent by 2030 ( based on the emissions levels in 2005 ) . <p> The Turnbull Government is aiming to have emissions from the electricity sector fall by the same proportion over the same period ? ? " 26 per cent by 2030 . <p> " I mean , we are just sick of all these caveats being placed on us by green groups . " <h> 3 . Target locked in for a decade <p> The NEG would lock in the 26 per cent target for emissions reduction for the electricity sector for 10 years , from 2020 to 2030 . <p> Mr Rattenbury wants more flexibility in the system , given his concerns that the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ compromise offered has been to review the target after five years , but Mr Rattenbury describes that as a " false olive branch " , given that provision had originally been included , then taken out , and is now being offered once again . <p> He questions the logic of locking in a 26 per cent target for the electricity sector when the overall national target of 26 per cent could change . <p> " It seems highly unlikely ? ? ? that will survive a change of Commonwealth government . " <p> " There will be a change because outside of the Coalition , no-one else supports this target . " <h> 4 .
